> AGENT

All three angles are in. On the **global allocator** question specifically — not as-is, but the path exists:

- `GlobalAlloc` demands `alloc(&self)` callable from **every thread concurrently**; our pool's whole phase-1 contract is one popper holding `&mut self`. Bridging that needs either the phase-2 shared-allocation pools (the generation-tagged head CAS we designed) or per-thread pools behind a routing layer — which is exactly how production mallocs are built (thread-local caches over shared slabs), so the arc is well-trodden.
- `GlobalAlloc` must serve **arbitrary `Layout`s**; a pool serves one size class. A real global allocator would be a small family of pools (64 B, 256 B, 4 KiB, …) with size-class routing and an oversize fallback.
- The part that already fits perfectly: `dealloc` from any thread is our MPSC free push, no adaptation needed.

So: feasible as a follow-on experiment, recorded as an Ideas entry with those constraints spelled out (and a note to measure the plain object-pool form in iiac-perf first — the numbers will say whether the malloc arc is worth walking).

README changes beyond that: the usage model's new **"Where this is heading"** block — a clearly-marked illustrative sketch of the composed flow (alloc → fill in place → `send` consumes the guard, index rides the ring → receiver resolves a zero-copy view → free returns it), closing with the one-sentence thesis: *bytes written once, never copied — not by send, not by receive, not across the process boundary*. Plus a Message-pool bullet making the standalone value explicit: a lock-free, syscall-free, O(1) fixed-size object allocator with LIFO cache reuse, usable today for objects that never travel.

validate_pool_geometry(buf_size: u32, buf_count: u32) -> Result<(), Error> {\n    if buf_size == 0 || !(buf_size as usize).is_multiple_of(CACHE_LINE) {\n        return Err(Error::BadBufSize);\n    }\n    if buf_count == 0 || buf_count == NIL {\n        return Err(Error::BadBufCount);\n    }\n    Ok(())\n}\n\n#[cfg(test)]\nmod tests {\n    use super::*;\n    use zerocopy::{FromBytes, Immutable, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};\n\n    /// Test message; two words so a torn write would be\n    /// visible.\n    #[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable, Debug, PartialEq)]\n    #[repr(C)]\n    struct Msg {\n        seq: u64,\n        val: u64,\n    }\n\n    /// Test region size: header + (4 buffers × 1 line each).\n    const REGION_BYTES: usize = size_of::<PoolHeader>() + (4 * CACHE_LINE);\n\n    /// Cache-line-aligned backing store for the tests' pools.\n    #[repr(C, align(64))]\n    struct Region([u8; REGION_BYTES]);\n\n    impl Region {\n        fn new() -> Self {\n            Region([0; REGION_BYTES])\n        }\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn init_rejects_bad_geometry() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        assert_eq!(\n            Pool::init(&mut r.0, 63, 4).err().unwrap(),\n            Error::BadBufSize\n        );\n        assert_eq!(Pool::init(&mut r.0, 0, 4).err().unwrap(), Error::BadBufSize);\n        assert_eq!(\n            Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 0).err().unwrap(),\n            Error::BadBufCount\n        );\n        assert_eq!(\n            Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, u32::MAX).err().unwrap(),\n            Error::BadBufCount\n        );\n        assert_eq!(Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 8).err().unwrap(), Error::TooSmall);\n        assert_eq!(\n            Pool::init(&mut r.0[1..], 64, 4).err().unwrap(),\n            Error::Misaligned\n        );\n        // 32-bit tripwire: size * count wraps a 32-bit usize\n        // (2^26 * 2^6 = 2^32); u64 region math must reject it.\n        assert_eq!(\n            Pool::init(&mut r.0, 1 << 26, 1 << 6).err().unwrap(),\n            Error::TooSmall\n        );\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn attach_validates_header() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        // Not initialized yet: magic is zero.\n        let err = unsafe { Pool::attach(r.0.as_mut_ptr(), r.0.len()) }\n            .err()\n            .unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(err, Error::BadMagic);\n        // A ring region is not a pool region: its magic must\n        // be rejected (the two kinds must never cross-attach).\n        crate::Ring::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n        let err = unsafe { Pool::attach(r.0.as_mut_ptr(), r.0.len()) }\n            .err()\n            .unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(err, Error::BadMagic);\n\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n        let pool = unsafe { Pool::attach(r.0.as_mut_ptr(), r.0.len()) }.unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(pool.buf_size(), 64);\n        assert_eq!(pool.buf_count(), 4);\n        // A region built with a different CACHE_LINE\n        // (simulated by editing the recorded value) is\n        // rejected.\n        pool.header.cache_line_size.store(128, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let err = unsafe { Pool::attach(r.0.as_mut_ptr(), r.0.len()) }\n            .err()\n            .unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(err, Error::BadCacheLine);\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn alloc_holds_many_then_exhausts() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n\n        // All four live at once — the decoupling: no guard\n        // blocks the next alloc.\n        let mut bufs = Vec::new();\n        for i in 0..4u64 {\n            let mut b = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n            b.seq = i;\n            b.val = i * 10;\n            bufs.push(b);\n        }\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n\n        // Distinct buffers: every write is still intact.\n        for (i, b) in bufs.iter().enumerate() {\n            assert_eq!(\n                **b,\n                Msg {\n                    seq: i as u64,\n                    val: i as u64 * 10\n                }\n            );\n        }\n        for b in bufs {\n            b.free();\n        }\n        // Fully recycled: four allocs succeed again.\n        let a = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let b = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let c = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let d = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n        a.free();\n        b.free();\n        c.free();\n        d.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn free_is_lifo() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n\n        let a = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let b = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let a_ptr = &*a as *const Msg;\n        let b_ptr = &*b as *const Msg;\n        a.free();\n        b.free(); // b freed last, so b is the stack top\n        let first = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let second = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(&*first as *const Msg, b_ptr);\n        assert_eq!(&*second as *const Msg, a_ptr);\n        first.free();\n        second.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn dropped_guard_leaks_its_buffer() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n\n        drop(pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap()); // leaked, not freed\n        // The pool now serves only three buffers.\n        let a = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let b = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let c = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n        a.free();\n        b.free();\n        c.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn corrupted_stack_fails_toward_exhausted() {\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n\n        // Peer scribbles an out-of-bounds head (not NIL).\n        pool.header.first_free_idx.store(1000, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n\n        // Valid head, corrupted next-link: still refused —\n        // the pop validates both before touching anything.\n        pool.header.first_free_idx.store(0, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        pool.next_buf_idx(0).store(77, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n\n        // Repairing the link restores service — degraded,\n        // never bricked.\n        pool.next_buf_idx(0).store(NIL, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let a = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Msg>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n        a.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn threaded_alloc_here_free_there() {\n        // Allocator thread allocs and hands guards to a freer\n        // thread; recycling means total allocations far\n        // exceed buf_count. Reduced under Miri (interpreted\n        // spin loops are slow).\n        const COUNT: u64 = if cfg!(miri) { 200 } else { 100_000 };\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, 64, 4).unwrap();\n\n        let (tx, rx) = std::sync::mpsc::channel::<BufSlot<'_, Msg>>();\n        std::thread::scope(|s| {\n            s.spawn(move || {\n                for i in 0..COUNT {\n                    loop {\n                        match pool.alloc::<Msg>() {\n                            Ok(mut b) => {\n                                b.seq = i;\n                                b.val = i * 3;\n                                tx.send(b).unwrap();\n                                break;\n                            }\n                            Err(Exhausted) => std::hint::spin_loop(),\n                        }\n                    }\n                }\n            });\n            s.spawn(move || {\n                for i in 0..COUNT {\n                    let b = rx.recv().unwrap();\n                    assert_eq!(b.seq, i);\n                    assert_eq!(b.val, i * 3);\n                    b.free(); // the freer role: not the allocator\n                }\n            });\n        });\n    }\n}"
```json
}
```

> AGENT

`0.6.0-4 test: message pool protocol tests` is ready to commit — 7 new tests in `pool.rs`, each pinned to a clause of the usage model, all passing under `cargo test` and full Miri (14 total with the ring's):

- **`init_rejects_bad_geometry`** — BadBufSize/BadBufCount/TooSmall/Misaligned, plus the 32-bit `size × count` wrap tripwire, mirroring the ring's.
- **`attach_validates_header`** — uninitialized → `BadMagic`; **a ring region → `BadMagic`** (the kinds can never cross-attach — a test only possible because the magics differ); good attach snapshots geometry; scribbled `cache_line_size` → `BadCacheLine`.
- **`alloc_holds_many_then_exhausts`** — the decoupling itself: four guards live at once with intact distinct contents, fifth alloc `Exhausted`, full recycle after frees.
- **`free_is_lifo`** — pointer-identity proof that the last freed buffer is the next allocated.
- **`dropped_guard_leaks_its_buffer`** — drop-without-free leaks exactly one buffer, pool serves `count-1` thereafter.
- **`corrupted_stack_fails_toward_exhausted`** — hostile head → `Exhausted`; valid head with hostile next-link → `Exhausted` (both validation arms); repaired link restores service — degraded, never bricked, never out-of-bounds.
- **`threaded_alloc_here_free_there`** — the role split live: allocator thread allocs and hands guards over a channel, freer thread verifies and frees; 100k messages through 4 buffers (200 under Miri), exercising the CAS push/pop pairing and `BufSlot: Send`.

One implementation addition surfaced by the threaded test: `Pool` itself now has `unsafe impl Send` (the allocator role moving to another thread), same rationale and placement as the ring endpoints' — the raw `*mut u8` field was blocking an otherwise-legitimate move.

Bookkeeping rode along: `[[18]]` backfill (`-3` SHA `9f3ce4bc…`), ladder `-4` done, version `0.6.0-4`. Ready for the description.

> DEVELOPER

Use a const for cache size in the tests, and test different sizes. Test different sized types in the same buffer and test that types with internal alignment work. Actually, how do we handle types that don't fit. What about allocating a zero-sized type?

> AGENT

Good push — those are exactly the API edges this cycle should pin down. Direct answers first, then the tests:

- **Types that don't fit**: `check_type` panics ("programming error, not runtime condition" — same doctrine as the ring's `reserve_slot`). That's currently only documented; I'll pin it with a `#[should_panic]` test.
- **ZSTs**: they're legal (`size 0 ≤ buf_size`, align 1) and *consume a whole buffer* — the buffer is the allocation granule. Weird but consistent; I'll document it on `alloc` and test it.

```json
  "new_string": "    #[test]\n    fn wider_buffers_and_mixed_types() {\n        // A two-line buffer pool: types of different sizes\n        // (and a type filling the buffer exactly) share it,\n        // and a freed buffer is reused as a different type.\n        const BIG_BUF: usize = 2 * CACHE_LINE;\n        #[repr(C, align(64))]\n        struct Region2([u8; size_of::<PoolHeader>() + 4 * BIG_BUF]);\n        let mut r = Region2([0; size_of::<PoolHeader>() + 4 * BIG_BUF]);\n\n        /// Fills the two-line buffer exactly.\n        #[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable)]\n        #[repr(C)]\n        struct Big {\n            words: [u64; BIG_BUF / 8],\n        }\n\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, BIG_BUF as u32, 4).unwrap();\n\n        // Different-sized types live in the pool at once.\n        let mut small = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        let mut big = pool.alloc::<Big>().unwrap();\n        small.seq = 7;\n        big.words[0] = 1;\n        big.words[BIG_BUF / 8 - 1] = 2; // last word: full span writable\n        assert_eq!(small.seq, 7);\n        assert_eq!(big.words[BIG_BUF / 8 - 1], 2);\n\n        // A freed buffer is reused as a different type.\n        let small_ptr = &*small as *const Msg as usize;\n        small.free();\n        let reused = pool.alloc::<Big>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(&*reused as *const Big as usize, small_ptr);\n        reused.free();\n        big.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn internally_aligned_types_work() {\n        /// Alignment 32 (≤ CACHE_LINE), no padding, so the\n        /// zerocopy derives accept it; the buffer base is\n        /// line-aligned, satisfying any align ≤ CACHE_LINE.\n        #[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable)]\n        #[repr(C, align(32))]\n        struct Aligned {\n            words: [u64; 4],\n        }\n        assert_eq!(core::mem::align_of::<Aligned>(), 32);\n\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, BUF, 4).unwrap();\n        let mut a = pool.alloc::<Aligned>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(&*a as *const Aligned as usize % 32, 0);\n        a.words = [1, 2, 3, 4];\n        assert_eq!(a.words[3], 4);\n        a.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    #[should_panic(expected = \"T larger than slot\")]\n    fn too_big_type_panics() {\n        /// Two lines: does not fit a one-line buffer.\n        #[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable)]\n        #[repr(C)]\n        struct TooBig {\n            words: [u64; 16],\n        }\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, BUF, 4).unwrap();\n        // A type that cannot fit is a programming error, not\n        // a runtime condition: alloc panics (check_type).\n        let _ = pool.alloc::<TooBig>();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn zero_sized_type_consumes_a_buffer() {\n        /// Zero bytes, alignment 1.\n        #[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable)]\n        #[repr(C)]\n        struct Zst;\n\n        let mut r = Region::new();\n        let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut r.0, BUF, 4).unwrap();\n        // Legal, and each one occupies a whole buffer — the\n        // buffer is the allocation granule.\n        let a = pool.alloc::<Zst>().unwrap();\n        let b = pool.alloc::<Zst>().unwrap();\n        let c = pool.alloc::<Zst>().unwrap();\n        let d = pool.alloc::<Zst>().unwrap();\n        assert_eq!(pool.alloc::<Zst>().err().unwrap(), Exhausted);\n        a.free();\n        b.free();\n        c.free();\n        d.free();\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn threaded_alloc_here_free_there() {"
}
```

> AGENT

All green — 18 tests under `cargo test` and full Miri. The four additions per your requests:

- **`wider_buffers_and_mixed_types`** — a 128-byte-buffer pool: a 16-byte `Msg` and a buffer-filling `Big` (`[u64; 16]`) live in the pool simultaneously, the full span of the wide buffer is writable, and a freed buffer is *reused as a different type* (pointer-identity checked). Buffer size expressed via `2 * CACHE_LINE`, no literals.
- **`internally_aligned_types_work`** — a `#[repr(C, align(32))]` type: allocates, pointer verified 32-aligned (any `align ≤ CACHE_LINE` is satisfied by construction since buffer bases are line-aligned), round-trips.
- **`too_big_type_panics`** — `#[should_panic]` pinning the doesn't-fit answer: a two-line type on a one-line pool panics in `check_type` at the `alloc` call.
- **`zero_sized_type_consumes_a_buffer`** — four ZST allocs exhaust a four-buffer pool: legal, granule-sized, documented now in `alloc`'s doc comment too.

Plus the const sweep: every literal `64` in the pool tests is now `BUF` (= `CACHE_LINE as u32`) or `CACHE_LINE` arithmetic; only the `#[repr(align(64))]` attributes keep the literal (attribute position can't name a const — same limitation the ring documents).

One small honesty note: the panic message the `should_panic` pins is `"T larger than slot"` — `check_type` is shared with the ring, so "slot" is the ring's word even in a pool context. Genericizing the message ("T larger than buffer/slot") is a two-line change if it bothers you; otherwise the doctrine and the test stand as-is. Ready for the description either way.

> AGENT

My thoughts: **`CACHE_LINE_SIZE`** wins, and here's the ranking logic:

- **`CACHE_LINE_SIZE`** — matches the header field we just renamed (`cache_line_size` records this constant; name symmetry makes that relationship self-evident), and "size" is Rust's word for bytes (`size_of`).
- **`CACHE_LINE_LEN`** — in Rust, `len` connotes element count (`slice.len()`); this is bytes, so `SIZE` over `LEN`.
- **`CLL`** — vetoed by the repo's own rule: AGENTS.md bans decoder-ring abbreviations ("Abbreviations (e.g. `SE`) are not used because they require a decoder ring"). Shoooort loses to readable here :)

And `TEST_CACHE_LINE` for the test const — one nuance: that const's actual role is the *buffer size* the tests pass to `Pool::init` (which happens to be one cache line). I'll name it as you asked; if it ever reads odd as `Pool::init(&mut r.0, TEST_CACHE_LINE, 4)`, the alternative is `TEST_BUF_SIZE`. Applying both renames:

```
test: message pool protocol tests

Eleven pool tests written against the README usage model —
geometry/attach validation, the decoupling itself (many
live guards), LIFO reuse, leak-on-drop, corruption failing
toward Exhausted, type edges (mixed sizes in one pool,
internal alignment, too-big panics, ZST consumes a
buffer), and a threaded allocator/freer role split. All
pass under cargo test and Miri. Naming: CACHE_LINE →
CACHE_LINE_SIZE crate-wide, matching the header fields.

- src/pool.rs
  - tests: init_rejects_bad_geometry (incl. 32-bit wrap
    tripwire), attach_validates_header (uninitialized and
    ring regions both BadMagic — kinds cannot
    cross-attach; BadCacheLine), alloc_holds_many_then_
    exhausts, free_is_lifo (pointer identity),
    dropped_guard_leaks_its_buffer,
    corrupted_stack_fails_toward_exhausted (head and
    next-link arms, repair restores service),
    wider_buffers_and_mixed_types (2-line buffers, reuse
    as a different type), internally_aligned_types_work
    (align(32)), too_big_type_panics (should_panic via
    check_type), zero_sized_type_consumes_a_buffer,
    threaded_alloc_here_free_there (guards cross a
    channel; 100k msgs through 4 buffers, 200 under Miri)
  - unsafe impl Send for Pool (allocator role moves across
    threads; surfaced by the threaded test)
  - alloc doc: geometry panics on mismatch; ZST legal,
    buffer is the allocation granule
  - TEST_CACHE_LINE_SIZE const replaces literal 64s in
    tests
- src/lib.rs: CACHE_LINE renamed CACHE_LINE_SIZE (pub
  const; all references)
```

> DEVELOPER

Although this is a library, I'd really like an example app that people can see it working, we could add that here or a 0.7.0 single commit, thoughts?

> AGENT

My recommendation: **not in the close-out** — the protocol makes close-out bookkeeping-only, and that discipline has been serving us — but as a **single-commit follow-on right after**, like the `0.4.1` and `0.5.1` precedents. So: push this close-out as staged, then the demo lands as its own small commit.

Two thoughts on the follow-on itself:

1. **Version**: an example app adds no library API, so by the repo's own instincts it's `0.6.1` rather than `0.7.0` (minor bumps have meant new crate surface — user line, pool). Happy to do `0.7.0` if you want the demo to *mark* the milestone, but `0.6.1` is my lean.
2. **Shape of the demo**: `examples/demo.rs` showing the honest current story end-to-end, with output people can run and read:
   - Two threads: a sender (pool **allocator** + ring **producer**) and a receiver (ring **consumer** + **freer**) — the exact role composition from the usage model.
   - Ring carries small by-value messages; pool buffers get allocated, filled, handed across, and freed — the get ≠ send decoupling visible (e.g. sender holds several allocated buffers while continuing to send).
   - A closing printout of messages moved and elapsed time — a crude msgs/sec so it doubles as a smoke-baseline you can eyeball before iiac-perf does it properly.
   - Listed in the README next to the other examples.

If that plan works: say go and I'll push the `0.6.0` close-out as approved, then build the demo as `0.6.1` (or tell me `0.7.0` and I'll use that).

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/examples/demo.rs",
  "content": "//! Demo: both primitives working across threads, with\n//! throughput printed — run `cargo run --release --example\n//! demo`.\n//!\n//! - Part 1, the ring: an SPSC pair moves typed messages\n//!   in place (reserve_slot → write → commit; reserve_slot\n//!   → read → release), one producer thread to one\n//!   consumer thread.\n//! - Part 2, the pool: an allocator thread allocs and\n//!   fills `BufSlot`s and hands them to a freer thread —\n//!   \"send\" today is moving the guard (see the README's\n//!   usage model); getting a buffer implies nothing about\n//!   when it is sent or freed.\n//! - The composed form — descriptors through the ring,\n//!   payloads at rest in pool buffers — is the next cycle;\n//!   this demo shows each primitive honestly on its own.\n\nuse std::time::Instant;\n\nuse zc_ring_x1::{BufSlot, CACHE_LINE_SIZE, Empty, Exhausted, Full, Pool, Ring};\nuse zerocopy::{FromBytes, Immutable, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};\n\n/// Messages moved per part.\nconst COUNT: u64 = 1_000_000;\n\n/// Ring slots / pool buffers (small on purpose: recycling\n/// under pressure is the interesting case).\nconst DEPTH: u32 = 64;\n\n/// The demo message; two words so a torn write would show.\n#[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout, Immutable, Debug, PartialEq)]\n#[repr(C)]\nstruct Msg {\n    seq: u64,\n    val: u64,\n}\n\n/// Region for either primitive: biggest header (ring, 4\n/// lines) + DEPTH one-line slots/buffers.\n#[repr(C, align(64))]\nstruct Region([u8; 4 * CACHE_LINE_SIZE + DEPTH as usize * CACHE_LINE_SIZE]);\n\n/// Move COUNT messages producer-thread → consumer-thread\n/// through the ring; return msgs/sec.\nfn ring_demo() -> f64 {\n    let mut region = Region([0; size_of::<Region>()]);\n    let (mut producer, mut consumer) = Ring::init(&mut region.0, CACHE_LINE_SIZE as u32, DEPTH)\n        .unwrap()\n        .split();\n\n    let start = Instant::now();\n    std::thread::scope(|s| {\n        s.spawn(move || {\n            for i in 0..COUNT {\n                loop {\n                    match producer.reserve_slot::<Msg>() {\n                        Ok(mut slot) => {\n                            slot.seq = i;\n                            slot.val = i * 3;\n                            slot.commit();\n                            break;\n                        }\n                        Err(Full) => std::hint::spin_loop(),\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n        });\n        s.spawn(move || {\n            for i in 0..COUNT {\n                loop {\n                    match consumer.reserve_slot::<Msg>() {\n                        Ok(msg) => {\n                            assert_eq!(msg.seq, i);\n                            msg.release();\n                            break;\n                        }\n                        Err(Empty) => std::hint::spin_loop(),\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n        });\n    });\n    COUNT as f64 / start.elapsed().as_secs_f64()\n}\n\n/// Alloc + fill COUNT messages on an allocator thread,\n/// free them on a freer thread (guards cross a std\n/// channel); return msgs/sec.\nfn pool_demo() -> f64 {\n    let mut region = Region([0; size_of::<Region>()]);\n    let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut region.0, CACHE_LINE_SIZE as u32, DEPTH).unwrap();\n\n    let (tx, rx) = std::sync::mpsc::sync_channel::<BufSlot<'_, Msg>>(DEPTH as usize);\n    let start = Instant::now();\n    std::thread::scope(|s| {\n        s.spawn(move || {\n            for i in 0..COUNT {\n                loop {\n                    match pool.alloc::<Msg>() {\n                        Ok(mut buf) => {\n                            buf.seq = i;\n                            buf.val = i * 3;\n                            tx.send(buf).unwrap();\n                            break;\n                        }\n                        Err(Exhausted) => std::hint::spin_loop(),\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n        });\n        s.spawn(move || {\n            for i in 0..COUNT {\n                let buf = rx.recv().unwrap();\n                assert_eq!(buf.seq, i);\n                buf.free();\n            }\n        });\n    });\n    COUNT as f64 / start.elapsed().as_secs_f64()\n}\n\n/// Run both parts and print their throughput.\nfn main() {\n    println!(\"zc-ring demo: {COUNT} messages each, depth {DEPTH}\");\n    println!(\"ring (SPSC, in-place):     {:>12.0} msgs/sec\", ring_demo());\n    println!(\"pool (alloc here → free there): {:>7.0} msgs/sec\", pool_demo());\n    println!(\"(composed descriptor flow arrives with the descriptor-queue cycle)\");\n}\n"
}
```

> AGENT

`0.6.1 feat: ring + pool demo example` is ready to commit:

- **`examples/demo.rs`** — both primitives live across threads, throughput printed. Ring part: 1M typed messages in place, producer thread → consumer thread, sequence-verified. Pool part: allocator thread allocs and fills `BufSlot`s, freer thread verifies and frees, guards crossing a bounded std channel exactly as the usage model defines "send" today. Closing line tells the visitor the composed descriptor flow is next. 12	//!   usage model); getting a buffer implies nothing about
```
13	//!   when it is sent or freed.
```

```json
{
```

```json
  "new_string": "/// Group a count into comma-separated thousands\n/// (`1234567` → `\"1,234,567\"`).\nfn commas(n: u64) -> String {\n    let digits = n.to_string();\n    let mut out = String::with_capacity(digits.len() + digits.len() / 3);\n    for (i, c) in digits.chars().enumerate() {\n        if i > 0 && (digits.len() - i).is_multiple_of(3) {\n            out.push(',');\n        }\n        out.push(c);\n    }\n    out\n}\n\n/// Print one part's line: msgs/sec (comma-grouped) and\n/// ns/msg from the part's elapsed seconds.\nfn report(label: &str, secs: f64) {\n    let rate = commas((COUNT as f64 / secs) as u64);\n    let ns_per_msg = secs * 1e9 / COUNT as f64;\n    println!(\"{label:<32} {rate:>12} msgs/sec  {ns_per_msg:>7.1} ns/msg\");\n}\n\n/// Move COUNT messages producer-thread → consumer-thread\n/// through the ring; return elapsed seconds.\nfn ring_demo() -> f64 {"
}
```

> DEVELOPER

I'm surpised getting a message is so expensive, sad really.

> AGENT

That 271 ns isn't the cost of getting a message — it's the cost of the *taxi ride* we put it in. The pool part's pipeline is alloc → **std channel send → recv** (with thread parking/unparking when it runs dry) → free; the channel hop is the dominant term, and it's exactly the piece the descriptor-queue cycle replaces with the 37 ns ring. Let me put a number on the pool itself so we're not guessing:

```json
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/examples/scratch_bench.rs",
  "content": "//! Throwaway micro-measure of raw pool alloc/free (not\n//! committed): single-threaded, no channel, no second\n//! thread — the pool's own cost in isolation.\n\nuse std::time::Instant;\nuse zc_ring_x1::{CACHE_LINE_SIZE, Pool};\nuse zerocopy::{FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout};\n\n/// Same message as the demo.\n#[derive(FromBytes, IntoBytes, KnownLayout)]\n#[repr(C)]\nstruct Msg {\n    seq: u64,\n    val: u64,\n}\n\n/// 64 one-line buffers behind a two-line header.\n#[repr(C, align(64))]\nstruct Region([u8; 2 * CACHE_LINE_SIZE + 64 * CACHE_LINE_SIZE]);\n\n/// Measure alloc→write→free round-trips, single thread.\nfn main() {\n    const N: u64 = 10_000_000;\n    let mut region = Region([0; size_of::<Region>()]);\n    let mut pool = Pool::init(&mut region.0, CACHE_LINE_SIZE as u32, 64).unwrap();\n\n    let start = Instant::now();\n    let mut sink = 0u64;\n    for i in 0..N {\n        let mut b = pool.alloc::<Msg>().unwrap();\n        b.seq = i;\n        b.val = i * 3;\n        sink = sink.wrapping_add(b.val);\n        b.free();\n    }\n    let ns = start.elapsed().as_secs_f64() * 1e9 / N as f64;\n    println!(\"alloc+write+free: {ns:.1} ns/msg (sink {sink})\");\n}\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Cheer up: **9.8 ns** for a full alloc → write → free round-trip (10M iterations, single-threaded, measured just now with a throwaway example that's already deleted). Call it ~5 ns to get a message and ~5 ns to free it — a pop and a push, two uncontended CASes, exactly as designed.

So the demo's 271 ns decomposes roughly as: ~10 ns of pool + ~260 ns of `std::sync::mpsc` — bounded-channel synchronization, thread parking/unparking, the works. The sad number is the standard library's messenger, not your allocator.

And that's precisely the thesis of the next cycle: replace the channel with the ring as the descriptor carrier and the projected composed path is alloc (~5) + ring transfer (~37) + free (~5) ≈ **~50 ns per message end-to-end, zero copies** — five times faster than the demo's current pool part, with the payload never moving at all.

Worth noting for later: when the descriptor queue lands, the demo will show that composition directly, and iiac-perf can pit it against `mpsc` head-to-head. If you'd like, next cycle could even print the decomposition (pool-only, ring-only, composed) as three demo lines — but that's a decision for when we open it. One piece of housekeeping also pending from the last push: the chores header and Done entry still say "demo example" while the commit title landed as "demo binary" — I'll sync those two lines in the next commit's notes edits.

> AGENT

Both loops are in — and the head-to-head is honest:

```
ring (SPSC, in-place):             14,031,664 msgs/sec     71.3 ns/msg
pool (alloc here -> free there):    3,064,577 msgs/sec    326.3 ns/msg
pool (alloc/free, 1 thread):       99,314,836 msgs/sec     10.1 ns/msg
global (Box::new/drop, 1 thread):  105,855,379 msgs/sec      9.4 ns/msg
```

Two observations worth having before you read too much into it:

- **Pool ≈ malloc single-threaded is the expected result, not a defeat.** glibc's fast path for a hot same-size allocation *is* a thread-local LIFO free-list — the same trick we built. The pool's case was never "beats malloc in a tight loop"; it's what malloc can't do at any speed: buffers in shared memory a peer can zero-copy read, `no_std`, no syscalls ever (malloc's tail latencies hide in arena locks and `brk`/`mmap`), and deterministic behavior for the embedded floor. Worth a line in the chores note so future-us doesn't misread the scoreboard.
- These are eyeball numbers with real run-to-run variance (the ring read 71 ns this run vs 37 ns earlier — same binary, thermal/scheduling noise). iiac-perf's calibrated harness remains the truth-teller.

`black_box` guards both loops against dead-code elimination. Bookkeeping done: title-sync fix landed (`demo example` → `demo binary` in the chores header, Done entry, and `[8]` anchor), `[[21]]` backfilled with the `0.6.1` SHA, version `0.6.2`. Clippy and tests clean.

Proposed description:

```
feat: demo alloc/free perf loops

Two single-thread loops join the demo scoreboard: the
pool's own alloc→write→free cost (no channel, no second
thread) and the same loop through the global allocator
(Box::new/drop). Pool ≈ malloc here is expected — malloc's
hot path is the same thread-local LIFO trick; the pool's
case is shared-memory zero-copy, no_std, and no syscalls,
not single-thread speed. black_box guards both loops.

- src/bin/zc-ring-x1-demo.rs: pool_alloc_free_demo,
  global_alloc_free_demo, two report lines
- notes/chores/chores-01.md: new section for this commit
  (empty Commits:); backfill [[21]] (the 0.6.1 SHA); sync
  the 0.6.1 section header to its landed title ("demo
  binary")
- notes/todo.md: Done entry [[9]]; [[8]] entry + anchor
  synced to "demo binary"
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: version-of-record 0.6.2
```

Your instinct about memory reuse is right but it applies to *both* sides — the interesting difference is in the instructions, not the memory. Let me verify my hypothesis with a 30-second experiment before claiming it:

> TOOL

tool_use Write
id: toolu_01MXaCWtFGzLouSHb8ZLXzZJ
```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/zc-ring-x1/examples/scratch_cas.rs",
  "content": "//! Throwaway (not committed): cost of two uncontended CAS\n//! ops per iteration — the pool's per-roundtrip atomic\n//! budget in isolation.\n\nuse std::sync::atomic::{AtomicU32, Ordering};\nuse std::time::Instant;\n\n/// Two compare_exchanges per iter, mirroring alloc + free.\nfn main() {\n    const N: u64 = 10_000_000;\n    let head = AtomicU32::new(0);\n    let start = Instant::now();\n    for _ in 0..N {\n        let h = head.load(Ordering::Acquire);\n        let _ = head.compare_exchange(h, h.wrapping_add(1), Ordering::Acquire, Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let h = head.load(Ordering::Relaxed);\n        let _ = head.compare_exchange(h, h.wrapping_sub(1), Ordering::Release, Ordering::Relaxed);\n    }\n    let ns = start.elapsed().as_secs_f64() * 1e9 / N as f64;\n    println!(\"2x uncontended CAS: {ns:.1} ns/iter\");\n    std::hint::black_box(head.load(Ordering::Relaxed));\n}\n"
}
```

> TOOL

tool_result

> AGENT

Hypothesis confirmed: **8.7 ns of our 9.9 ns is just the two CASes.** The pool's round-trip is one `compare_exchange` to pop (alloc) and one to push (free), and on x86 a `lock cmpxchg` costs ~18–25 cycles *even with zero contention* — the lock prefix's ordering guarantees aren't free. Everything else we do (bounds checks, link read, the 16-byte write) fits in the remaining ~1 ns.

Now the other side: glibc's malloc fast path (tcache) is a **thread-local** free list — plain loads and stores, *zero atomic instructions*. Your "same memory each time" intuition is correct and it's true for both allocators (both LIFO-reuse the same hot chunk, L1-resident); the difference is that malloc's bookkeeping is private by construction while ours is shared by design. We pay the "a peer in another process might free into this pool right now" tax on every single operation, even in a single-threaded loop where no peer exists. Same-speed-as-malloc means the entire cost of being cross-process-capable is two locked instructions — honestly, a good deal.

And there's a well-trodden road to beating malloc if we ever want it, because malloc itself shows the trick: **put a private cache in front of the shared structure** — exactly tcache-over-arenas:

- The allocator handle keeps a small thread-private list; alloc/free hit it with plain load/store (~1–2 ns).
- Refill/flush moves buffers between the private list and the shared CAS stack **in batches** — one CAS amortized over N messages instead of per message. (Batch-pop of a linked stack does need care — you can't atomically take N nodes without walking — but batch *push* is one CAS, and refill can pop a few at a time.)
- The shared free-stack stays the cross-process truth; the cache is pure per-handle optimization, invisible to the protocol.

That's a real design (it's also step one on the `#[global_allocator]` road already in Ideas), but I'd hold it until iiac-perf shows the per-op CAS actually matters in a composed workload — in the messaging path, ~9 ns of pool sits next to ~40 ns of ring transfer, so the cache would optimize the smaller term. Want me to record the private-cache idea (with today's 8.7 ns CAS measurement as the motivating datum) as an Ideas entry in this commit?

> DEVELOPER

Yes, add the private-cache idea, then push. I'm not worried about it, and we're still pretty fast and our p99 and stddev should be much much better :)
